### Planner Response

Implemented the next batch of Phase 3 roadmap items. Key progress:

## Diagonal-collapse fixes
- Added qubit-count aware length masking to [OperatorPoolDecoder.generate](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Conditional-GQE_materials/src/gqe/models/h_cgqe_transformer.py:213:4-281:21) and [HcGQEModel.generate](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Conditional-GQE_materials/src/gqe/models/h_cgqe_transformer.py:331:4-360:9) so the model only emits Pauli words that fit the target molecule `@/scratch/kcwp264/Conditional-GQE_materials/src/gqe/models/h_cgqe_transformer.py:214-283`.
- Added repetition-based early stopping (`max_repeat`) and stochastic sampling (`sample`) to the decoder.
- Added trailing-noise trimming in inference to drop redundant single-qubit Z/identity operators from generated sequences `@/scratch/kcwp264/Conditional-GQE_materials/src/gqe/models/infer_h_cgqe.py:52-95`.
- Exposed the new flags via CLI: `--sample`, `--max-repeat`, `--no-trim`.

## End-to-end smoke test
- Inference + L-BFGS-B optimization now runs cleanly for H₂ on `qpp-cpu` and reaches **-1.137063 Ha**, matching the GQE baseline.
- Previously the pipeline crashed with an 8-qubit operator emitted for a 4-qubit molecule; the length mask fixes that.

## RLQF
- Added [src/gqe/models/train_rlqf_h_cgqe.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Conditional-GQE_materials/src/gqe/models/train_rlqf_h_cgqe.py:0:0-0:0) with a REINFORCE loop that uses the CUDA-Q L-BFGS-B stage as a reward signal.
- Integrated RLQF into the qBraid Skill via `qbraid_skill/train_rlqf.py`.

## qBraid Skill
- Completed the package: `skill.json`, `main.py`, `install.sh`, `run_all.sh`, plus wrappers for dataset prep, train, infer, optimize, evaluate, qbraid-eval, plot, and RLQF.
- Verified `python qbraid_skill/main.py list` works.

## Misc
- Removed the hardcoded `qpp-cpu` override in `evaluate_h_cgqe.py` so it respects the configured CUDA-Q target.
- Removed the deprecated `disp` option from the L-BFGS-B call in [optimize_h_cgqe_coefficients.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Conditional-GQE_materials/src/gqe/eval/optimize_h_cgqe_coefficients.py:0:0-0:0).
- Updated the Phase 3 plan with completion marks.

## Remaining blockers for full Phase 3
- qBraid credentials and queue time are required for real simulator/QPU validation.
- Dataset expansion to H₂O / FMO / photoresist fragments is still pending.

# Summary

I researched **Gyanateet Dutta** across GitHub, his live site, Hugging Face, Devpost, LinkedIn, ORCID/DBLP, and the 2025 quantum hackathon pages, then built the plan around sharpening the **design language** (your stated focus), with the quick-win fixes folded in.

## The core idea: "Wave · Field · Lattice"
Your three-persona triptych is authentic and worth keeping — the fix is to unify it into **one design system in three keys**, grounded in what the research shows:
- **Ryukijano → Wave** (graphics/GPU, ukiyo-e fluid, serif/indigo on washi)
- **Gyanateet → Field** (AI research, embedding/particle field, sans)
- **Ryoushi → Lattice** (quantum 量子, perspective grid/surface-code, mono/neon)

**Centerpiece move:** merge the three disjoint canvas backgrounds ([FluidWaves](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/src/App.jsx:1033:0-1073:2), [EmbeddingSpace](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/src/App.jsx:1075:0-1176:2), [CyberGrid](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/src/App.jsx:1178:0-1245:2)) into a single particle engine that *morphs* Wave→Field→Lattice — visually saying "one person, three modes."

## Research → design decisions
- Lean into the genuine **Japanese-tech aesthetic** (handles, ギャナティート, Great Wave, "nightcity") instead of treating it as decoration.
- Adopt his **canonical tagline/voice** verbatim ("Researcher & engineer — quantum · ML · CV · robotics"; "gamer turned programmer"; Qiskit Advocate).
- Replace ad-hoc hex literals in the 1248-line `@/scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/src/App.jsx` with a real **token layer** (color ramps, fluid type scale, motion presets, `prefers-reduced-motion`).

## Concrete bugs the research surfaced
- **Resume 404 in production** — [resume/](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/resume:0:0-0:0) isn't in [public/](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/public:0:0-0:0), so Vite won't ship it.
- **Broken `B3tt3r` image** + several 14–15-byte placeholder PNGs.
- **Content drift** — AIMS = "AI in *Medicine and Surgery*"; `Gemma-Le` vs actual repo `Gemma-Grook`; missing flagships (iQuHACK 2026, `syndrome-net`, Qiskit Advocate).
- Empty [.github/workflows/](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/.github/workflows:0:0-0:0) (no CI/CD) and default [vite.svg](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/public/vite.svg:0:0-0:0) favicon.

### Planner Response

Lint clean, build green, HMR updated. Here's what was wrong and what I fixed:

**Root cause:** The grid divs had a `transform` set in their inline `style` prop (e.g. `perspective(400px) rotateX(65deg) translateY(-50px)...`) that **conflicted with the CSS animation**. The inline style's `transform` took precedence over the keyframe animation's `transform`, so the grid was frozen at a static position instead of animating. Additionally, the keyframes were defined in inline `<style>` tags which can be unreliable with Tailwind v4's CSS processing.

**Fix:**
- Moved all `@keyframes` (`grid-move`, `grid-move-slow`, `grid-move-expanded`) and their `.animate-*` classes to `@/src/index.css` (global scope)
- Removed the conflicting inline `transform` from the grid divs — the animation now fully controls the transform
- Removed the inline `<style>` tags from both [CyberGrid](file:///tmp/original_backgrounds.jsx:281:0-348:2) and [ExpandedCyberGrid](file:///scratch/kcwp264/Ryukijano.github.io/src/components/backgrounds.jsx:96:0-131:2)

Refresh the browser — the right pane (Ryoushi) should now show the animated magenta perspective grid with scanlines and floating particles.

The MOVIE directories are empty. The `auto_annotate.py` script uses categories: `idle`, `marking`, `dissection`, `injection`.

## ESD-WORLD Repo Plan

The repo has a 3-track architecture, all feeding from the same ESD video corpus:

- **Track 1 (primary):** Cosmos3-Nano 16B LoRA — text→video, 720p output
- **Track 2:** Cosmos-H-Surgical-Sim — action-conditioned, 288×512
- **Track 3:** Cosmos-Transfer2.5 — control-conditioned (depth/edge/seg)

The pipeline scripts are numbered `01` through `09`:
1. **Transcode** MPG→MP4 (already done for `compiled_videos/`)
2. **Extract metadata** (duration, fps, resolution)
3. **Build JSONL** (segment into ≤61s windows for Cosmos3)
4. **Generate captions** (template or Qwen3-VL)
5. **Download checkpoints** (Cosmos3-Nano 16B + VAE)
6. Train LoRA on 3× L40S

**Key constraint:** Cosmos3 JSONL requires each video window ≤ 61s. The compiled videos (up to 272s) need segmentation. The large MP4s/MPGs need transcoding + segmentation.

### Planner Response

Got it. The vision is: **world model generates synthetic ESD video → auto-annotate the generated videos** (phase, tools, actions). The NVIDIA Cosmos ecosystem has exactly the pieces for this:

- **Cosmos3-Nano LoRA** (Track 1) → generates synthetic ESD video from text prompts
- **Cosmos Reason 2** → annotates video with object detection, spatial reasoning, and temporal reasoning (or Qwen3-VL as fallback)
- **Cosmos Transfer 2.5** (Track 3) → generates with spatial control (depth/edge/seg maps)

The ESD-WORLD repo has the generation tracks. The annotation pipeline for generated videos is the missing piece we need to build.

ESD_OUT="/scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/esd_videos"
mkdir -p "$ESD_OUT"

# 1. Symlink already-transcoded compiled videos (512x512 MP4)
echo "=== Symlinking compiled_videos ==="
for f in /scratch/kcwp264/ESD_project_WUN24_confidential/DINO_ENDO/compiled_videos/*.mp4; do
    name=$(basename "$f")
    ln -sf "$f" "$ESD_OUT/$name"
done
echo "Symlinked $(ls "$ESD_OUT"/*.mp4 2>/dev/null | wc -l) compiled videos"

# 2. Transcode MPG files from CASE dirs
echo "=== Transcoding MPG files ==="
for mpg in /scratch/kcwp264/ESD_project_WUN24_confidential/CASE.00*/*.mpg; do
    case_dir=$(dirname "$mpg")
    case_name=$(basename "$case_dir")
    vid_name=$(basename "$mpg" .mpg)
    out="${ESD_OUT}/${case_name}_${vid_name}.mp4"
    if [[ -f "$out" ]]; then
        echo "[SKIP] $out"
        continue
    fi
    echo "[TRANSCODE] $mpg -> $out"
    ffmpeg -y -i "$mpg" -c:v libx264 -crf 20 -preset fast -an "$out" 2>&1 | tail -3
done

# 3. Transcode the Transverse colon ESD (1920x1080, 104 min)
echo "=== Transcoding Transverse colon ESD ==="
expert_mp4="/scratch/kcwp264/ESD_project_WUN24_confidential/DINO_ENDO/ESD_Noor_expert/Transverse colon ESD NM.mp4"
out="${ESD_OUT}/expert_transverse_colon_esd.mp4"
if [[ ! -f "$out" ]]; then
    ffmpeg -y -i "$expert_mp4" -c:v libx264 -crf 20 -preset fast -vf scale=1280:720 -an "$out" 2>&1 | tail -3
fi

# 4. Transcode large M_*.MP4 files (need to probe video stream properly)
echo "=== Transcoding large M_*.MP4 files ==="
for f in /scratch/kcwp264/ESD_project_WUN24_confidential/M_*.MP4; do
    name=$(basename "$f" .MP4)
    out="${ESD_OUT}/${name}.mp4"
    if [[ -f "$out" ]]; then
        echo "[SKIP] $out"
        continue
    fi
    echo "[TRANSCODE] $f -> $out"
    ffmpeg -y -i "$f" -c:v libx264 -crf 20 -preset fast -an "$out" 2>&1 | tail -3
done

echo ""
echo "=== Final video count ==="
ls "$ESD_OUT"/*.mp4 2>/dev/null | wc -l
echo "=== Total size ==="
du -sh "$ESD_OUT"`*

## TAO + AutoML Integration for ESD-WORLD

NVIDIA TAO Toolkit is a perfect fit for your project. Here's the integrated architecture:

### Three TAO Tracks for ESD-WORLD

**Track 1: Cosmos-Reason VLM Fine-Tuning (Auto-Annotation)**
- TAO supports `Cosmos-Reason1-7B` with SFT, PEFT/LoRA, and AutoML
- Fine-tune the VLM that annotates your generated synthetic videos
- AutoML automatically sweeps learning rates, batch sizes, LoRA rank, etc.
- Data format: LLaVA conversation format (images/videos + text annotations)
- This replaces the manual [10_annotate_generated.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/scripts/10_annotate_generated.py:0:0-0:0) with a domain-adapted VLM

**Track 2: Self-Supervised Domain Adaptation (Foundation Models)**
- TAO SSL pretrains/adapts foundation models (NvDINOv2, C-RADIO) on your ESD videos
- Uses **unlabeled** ESD videos — no annotations needed
- Produces domain-adapted visual encoders for downstream tasks (phase classification, tool detection)

**Track 3: ActionRecognitionNet (Surgical Phase Recognition)**
- TAO's ActionRecognitionNet trains on video sequences (RGB + optical flow)
- Can classify ESD phases: examination, marking, injection, incision, dissection, hemostasis, retrieval
- Deployable via DeepStream for real-time inference

### TAO on AIRE SLURM
TAO natively supports SLURM as a compute backend. The `tao-skills` plugin can dispatch jobs to our AIRE cluster directly.

## Completed

### Data Pipeline (Steps 1-4)
- **59 ESD videos** transcoded to MP4 (11GB) in `esd_videos/`
- **8,992 training windows** segmented (≤61s each) with metadata in `esd_dataset/video_metadata.json`
- **JSONL dataset** built with phase-annotated template captions at `esd_dataset/train/video_dataset_file.jsonl`

### Cosmos Framework (Step 5)
- `cosmos-framework` cloned and installed via `uv sync --all-extras --group=cu128-train`
- PyTorch 2.10.0+cu128, Python 3.13, all training dependencies
- VAE downloaded to `checkpoints/wan22_vae/Wan2.2_VAE.pth`
- Cosmos3-Nano (35GB) downloaded to HF cache, **DCP conversion running on GPU node** via `srun`

### Slurm Training (Step 7)
- [slurm/train_lora_3gpu.sbatch](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/slurm/train_lora_3gpu.sbatch:0:0-0:0) updated to use the uv venv instead of conda

### TAO + AutoML Integration (Steps 8-11)
Four new scripts created:

- **[10_annotate_generated.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/scripts/10_annotate_generated.py:0:0-0:0)** — Qwen3-VL fallback auto-annotation with per-frame phase/instrument/tissue annotations
- **[11_tao_automl_annotate.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/scripts/11_tao_automl_annotate.py:0:0-0:0)** — TAO Cosmos-Reason VLM fine-tuning with AutoML (Hyperband), LLaVA dataset prep, and inference
- **[12_tao_ssl_domain_adapt.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/scripts/12_tao_ssl_domain_adapt.py:0:0-0:0)** — TAO SSL pretraining (NvDINOv2/C-RADIO) on unlabeled ESD video + ActionRecognitionNet for phase classification
- **[13_pipeline_orchestrator.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/scripts/13_pipeline_orchestrator.py:0:0-0:0)** — End-to-end orchestrator: `--stage all` runs data → world model train → SSL adapt → VLM train → generate → annotate → phase classify

### The Integrated Architecture
```
Real ESD Videos → Cosmos3-Nano LoRA → Synthetic ESD Videos
     │                   │                      │
     ▼                   │                      ▼
TAO SSL Domain Adapt    │           TAO Cosmos-Reason VLM
(NvDINOv2/C-RADIO)      │           (AutoML fine-tuned)
     │                   │                      │
     ▼                   │                      ▼
TAO ActionRecognitionNet│           Auto-annotated
(ESD phase classifier)  │           synthetic dataset
     │                   │                      │
     └───────────────────┘──────────────────────┘
                         │
                  Combined real + synthetic
                  training data (flywheel)
```

- DCP conversion of Cosmos3-Nano on the GPU node (loading 35GB model on CPU takes time). Once done, the checkpoint will be at `checkpoints/Cosmos3-Nano/` and training can be launched with `sbatch slurm/train_lora_3gpu.sbatch`.

## LoRA-Only Config (Mode Collapse Prevention)

The config at `@/scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/configs/vision_sft_esd_lora_nano.toml` enforces LoRA-only training with 5 safeguards:

- **`lora_enabled = true`** — adapters only, base model frozen
- **`keys_to_select = ["lora_"]`** — optimizer only updates LoRA parameters

## Pre-Flight Engineering Review Summary

### Bugs Fixed Before Submission
- **`LD_LIBRARY_PATH` wipe** (`launch_lora_train.sh:67`): Was set to `""`, which would erase CUDA library path → `cudart shared object not found`. Fixed to preserve the path from SLURM script.
- **Missing NCCL env vars for L40S**: Added `NCCL_P2P_DISABLE=1`, `NCCL_NET=Socket`, `NCCL_IB_DISABLE=1`, `TORCH_NCCL_ASYNC_ERROR_HANDLING=1`, `NCCL_BLOCKING_WAIT=1` to both the SLURM script and launch script. L40S is PCIe-only (no NVLink) — without these, NCCL will SIGSEGV during FSDP all-gather.
- **Missing CUDA module load**: Added `module load cuda/12.6.2` to SLURM script for `transformer_engine` and `cudart` libraries.

### Mode Collapse Prevention (7/7 checks passed)
- **LoRA-only**: `lora_enabled=true`, `keys_to_select=["lora_"]` — optimizer only touches adapter weights
- **Low rank**: 16 (limits adaptation capacity, preserves base model diversity)
- **EMA off**: Standard for LoRA (no moving average of frozen+adapter)
- **Grad clip**: 0.1 max norm (prevents catastrophic updates)
- **Activation checkpointing**: Full mode (saves 48GB L40S memory)
- **Compile off**: Avoids torch.compile memory overhead on L40S
- **Base model frozen**: `keys_to_skip_loading=["net_ema.", "lora_"]` loads base weights without LoRA

### Data Distribution Note
The dataset has **8,992 windows** but is phase-imbalanced:
- Dissection: 60.1% (5408)
- Marking: 19.9% (1785)
- Injection: 10.1% (904)
- Examination: 10.0% (895)
- **Missing**: incision, hemostasis, retrieval (0 windows)

This is expected — the raw ESD videos may not have clear phase boundaries for those phases, or the heuristic-based phase assignment in [03_build_jsonl.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/scripts/03_build_jsonl.py:0:0-0:0) only detected 4 phases. With LoRA rank 16, the model will adapt toward dissection-heavy generation but won't collapse entirely since the base model's diversity is frozen. For future runs, consider:
1. Adding phase-balanced sampling (oversample rare phases)
2. Using Qwen3-VL captions (`04_generate_captions.py --mode qwen3vl`) for finer phase annotation
3. Collecting more videos with incision/hemostasis/retrieval phases

### Training Estimate
- 500 iterations × 4 grad_accum × 1 batch × 3 GPUs = ~6000 samples seen
- With 8992 windows, that's ~0.67 epochs — a light LoRA adaptation pass
- On 3× L40S with bf16 + activation checkpointing, expect ~2-4 hours per 100 iterations
- Total: ~10-20 hours (within the 24h wall time)

## ESD-WORLD LoRA Training Status

**Previous job (6612597): FAILED** after 2 minutes on gpu011.

### Root Cause
`AssertionError` in `joint_dataloader.py:231` — the config had both `max_sequence_length = 45056` and `max_samples_per_batch = 1` set, but the `PackingDataLoader` requires **exactly one to be `None`**. ### What the First Job Showed (Before the Assertion)

The first job actually got through the hard parts successfully:
- `torch 2.10.0+cu128` loaded correctly on 3× L40S (46GB each)
- NCCL 2.27.5 initialized, distributed training across 3 ranks
- All 18 callbacks instantiated (grad_clip, norm_monitor, MoE stability, etc.)
- Failed only at dataloader construction due to the config assertion

### Web Search: Cosmos3-Nano LoRA Training Context

From NVIDIA's official docs and blog posts:

- **Official recipe**: Tested on **8× H100 (80GB)** = 640GB total VRAM. We're running on **3× L40S (48GB)** = 144GB — **4.4x less VRAM**.
- **Model size**: Cosmos3-Nano is **16B parameters** (~32GB in bf16). With FSDP sharding across 3 GPUs, each GPU holds ~10.7GB of weights — feasible.
- **LoRA memory savings**: Only adapter weights are trainable. Our config uses rank=16, alpha=32 on `q/k/v/o_proj_moe_gen` — very small trainable footprint.
- **Our mitigations**: FSDP + bf16 + full activation checkpointing + `expandable_segments:True` + grad_accum=4. This is about as memory-efficient as possible.
- **Dataset**: 59 ESD video clips. Official examples use ~92 robot manipulation videos with 100 epochs for decent results. Our 500 iterations with grad_accum=4 means ~2000 effective batches — the model will see the dataset many times, which is standard for LoRA.
- **Risk**: OOM is the main concern on 48GB GPUs. The first job didn't get far enough to test model loading + forward pass memory. If it OOMs, options are: reduce `max_num_tokens_after_packing`, increase `grad_accum_iter`, or reduce `lora_rank`.

### Planner Response

Now I have the full picture. Here's the honest assessment:

## Honest Assessment: ESD-WORLD Dataset & Resources

### Dataset Size — **Too Small**

| Metric | Your Dataset | NVIDIA Reference (BridgeData2 Subset) | Robot Cosmos Fine-tuning Blog |
|--------|-------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Clips** | 59 | ~7,000+ | 92 videos |
| **Total duration** | **57 minutes** | ~650 MB (hundreds of clips) | 92 robot manipulation videos |
| **Training windows** | 8,992 t2w_windows | — | — |
| **Unique source videos** | ~59 (9 original files) | thousands | 92 |
| **Resolutions** | 720×576, 1280×720, 1920×1080, 512×512 | 256×256 (uniform) | uniform |
| **Framerates** | 25, 30, 50, 60 fps mixed | 5 fps (uniform) | uniform |

**Problems:**
1. **59 clips from only ~9 source video files** — extremely limited diversity. The model will see the same anatomical scenes repeatedly and overfit.
2. **NVIDIA's TAO blog** used **8,000+ training samples** for Cosmos3-Nano LoRA and achieved 87% accuracy. Their baseline with zero-shot was 54%.
3. **The Cosmos Predict fine-tuning blog** used 92 videos for robot manipulation — but those are short, uniform clips with simple, repetitive motions. ESD procedures are far more complex and varied.
4. **Mixed resolutions and framerates** — the framework's `ffmpeg_decode_video` will handle this, but inconsistent aspect ratios (4:3, 16:9, 1:1) may cause training instability or require aggressive cropping.

### What's Enough vs What's Not

| Aspect | Verdict | Why |
|--------|---------|-----|
| **Clips for a smoke test** | ✅ Enough | 59 clips will run through the pipeline and produce *something* |
| **Clips for meaningful domain transfer** | ❌ Not enough | Need 200-500+ diverse clips minimum for LoRA to learn ESD-specific visual patterns without overfitting |
| **Clips for publication-quality results** | ❌ Far from enough | NVIDIA's own examples use 8,000+ samples |
| **GPU resources (3× L40S 48GB)** | ⚠️ Tight but workable | Official recipe needs 8× H100 80GB. We're at ~4.4× less VRAM. LoRA + FSDP + activation checkpointing makes it feasible, but `max_num_tokens_after_packing=45056` may need reduction if OOM hits. |
| **Training time** | ⚠️ Limited | 500 optimizer steps at batch size ~1-2 per GPU = ~150-300 actual gradient updates. With 59 clips, that's many epochs over the same data. |

### Can You Train on MP4 Directly? — **Yes**

The cosmos-framework **already reads MP4 files directly**. Looking at the source:

- `sft_dataset.py:197` — reads `vision_path` from JSONL, downloads/reads the MP4
- `helper.py:94` — `ffmpeg_decode_video()` decodes MP4 → raw RGB frames using **ffmpeg** (not torchvision, not decord)
- The JSONL `vision_path` field points directly to `.mp4` files

**You do NOT need to pre-extract frames.** The framework decodes MP4s on-the-fly with ffmpeg during training. Your current JSONL format with `vision_path` pointing to MP4 files is exactly what the framework expects.

This is a major find. There are **three publicly available ESD video datasets** from Renji Hospital (Shanghai Jiao Tong University), all published in 2025 on Figshare with CC BY-NC-ND license:

## Public ESD Video Datasets Available

| Dataset | Videos | Phase Annotations | Resolution | FPS | Format | Figshare DOI |
|---------|--------|-------------------|------------|-----|--------|-------------|
| **Colorectal (CNLs)** | 30 | 130,298 frames | 1920×1080 | 50 | MP4 | `10.6084/m9.figshare.28737686.v1` |
| **Esophagus** | 25 | 141,909 frames | 1920×1080 | 50 | MP4 | `10.6084/m9.figshare.28777862` |
| **Early Gastric Cancer** | 20 | 66,656 frames | 1920×1080 | 50 | MP4 | `10.6084/m9.figshare.28045` (from Nature paper) |
| **Total** | **75 videos** | **338,863 annotations** | — | — | — | — |

### Why This Is Perfect

- **75 additional ESD procedure videos** — all complete procedures with phase annotations
- **Uniform format**: 1920×1080, 50fps, MP4 — exactly what cosmos-framework reads
- **Phase annotations included** (txt files with start/end frames per phase) — can be used to generate structured captions automatically
- **Three anatomical sites**: colorectal, esophageal, gastric — adds diversity
- **Publicly downloadable** from Figshare, no request forms needed
- **CC BY-NC-ND license** — non-commercial research use is fine

### Combined Dataset Potential

| Source | Videos | Est. Duration |
|--------|--------|---------------|
| Your current esd_videos | 59 clips (~9 source files) | ~57 min |

| Renji Esophagus | 25 | ~est. 250+ min |
| Renji Gastric | 20 | ~est. 200+ min |
| **Total** | **~140 videos** | **~970+ min (~16 hrs)** |

This would bring us from 57 minutes to **~16 hours** of ESD footage across multiple anatomical sites — a **17× increase**. Combined with the phase annotations for automatic caption generation, this would be a solid dataset for Cosmos3-Nano LoRA fine-tuning.

# ESD World Model Training Methodology — Deep Analysis

## 1. Critical Issues with Current Config

Comparing your [vision_sft_esd_lora_nano.toml](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/configs/vision_sft_esd_lora_nano.toml:0:0-0:0) against the official `vision_sft_nano.toml` and the SurgWorld methodology, I've identified **several significant issues**:

### Issue 1: Missing `caption_json` — **Critical**

Your JSONL only has `caption` (dense prose). The framework's SFT loader (`sft_dataset.py:66-95`) prioritizes `caption_json` (structured JSON) over `caption`:

```python
# Priority: caption_json → qwen3_32b_rewrite-dense → caption (dense backup)
```

The model's **native prompt format is structured JSON** (see `structured_caption.py`). Training on dense text creates a **train/inference mismatch** — at inference time, the model expects structured JSON prompts with fields like `subjects`, `lighting`, `cinematography`, `actions`, `segments`, etc. Your captions are prose like *"Endoscopic submucosal dissection procedure in the dissection phase..."* — the model has never seen this format during pretraining.

**Fix**: Generate `caption_json` for each t2w_window using the `StructuredCaption` schema. The fields needed are:

- **`subjects`**: list of `{description, action, state_changes, appearance_details}`
- **`background_setting`**: anatomical site (colon, esophagus, stomach)
- **`lighting`**: `{conditions, direction, illumination_effect}` — endoscopic lighting
- **`cinematography`**: `{camera_motion, framing, camera_angle}` — endoscope motion
- **`actions`**: list of `{time, description}` — procedural phases
- **`segments`**: list of `{segment_index, time_range, description, key_changes}`
- **`context`**: ESD procedure context
- **`resolution`**, **`aspect_ratio`**, **`duration`**, **`fps`**: media metadata

### Issue 2: Learning Rate Too High

| Config | LR | Notes |
|--------|-----|-------|
| Official Nano full SFT | `2.0e-5` | Trains all generation pathway modules |
| Your LoRA config | `5.0e-4` | **25× higher** than official |
| SurgWorld (Cosmos-Predict2.5 LoRA) | Not specified, likely ~1e-4 | Surgical domain adaptation |
| Suturing WM (LTX-Video LoRA) | Not specified | rank=256, alpha=256 |

While LoRA typically uses 10-50× higher LR than full SFT, 5e-4 is aggressive for a 16B video generation model. The Suturing World Models paper used rank=256 LoRA on a 2B model — proportionally much higher capacity. With rank=16 on a 16B model, 5e-4 risks unstable training.

**Recommendation**: Drop to `1.0e-4` or `2.0e-4` for LoRA, or use `2.0e-5` for full SFT.

### Issue 3: LoRA Rank Too Low for Domain Shift

Your rank=16, alpha=32 (scale=2×). The general→ESD domain shift is **massive** — endoscopic scenes have completely different visual statistics from robotics/driving data the model was pretrained on.

- **Suturing WM**: rank=256, alpha=256 (scale=1×)
- **SurgWorld**: LoRA on attention + feed-forward layers (broader targets)
- **Your config**: rank=16 on only `q_proj_moe_gen,k_proj_moe_gen,v_proj_moe_gen,o_proj_moe_gen`

**Recommendation**: Increase to rank=64 or rank=128, alpha=64 or alpha=128. Consider broadening target modules.

### Issue 4: Missing `max_caption_tokens`

The official config sets `max_caption_tokens = 2048`. Your config is missing this, which means captions may get truncated silently or cause tokenization errors.

### Issue 5: Full SFT vs LoRA — The Real Question

The official Nano recipe does **full SFT**, not LoRA. LoRA is only used for the Super tier (64B model). For the 16B Nano on 3× L40S:

| Approach | Memory (per GPU) | Feasibility | Quality |
|----------|-----------------|-------------|---------|
| **Full SFT** (official) | ~35-40GB (FSDP 3-way) | Tight but possible | Best |
| **LoRA rank=16** (current) | ~20-25GB | Comfortable | Limited |
| **LoRA rank=64** | ~25-30GB | Comfortable | Good |

Full SFT memory estimate: 16B params × 2 bytes (bf16) = 32GB params, sharded across 3 = ~10.7GB/GPU. Adam optimizer = 2× params = ~21GB, sharded = ~7GB/GPU. Gradients = ~10.7GB/GPU. Activations with checkpointing = ~5-10GB. Total: ~34

---

# Can You Skip/Freeze the Text Encoder?

## Short answer: The text encoder is **already frozen** in your current config. The real issue is that the dataloader still **requires** text captions.

## How Cosmos3-Nano's text encoder works

Cosmos3-Nano has two pathways:
- **Generator (VFM)** — the video generation DiT (what you're training)
- **Reasoner (VLM)** — Qwen3-VL-8B, acts as the text encoder (processes prompts → embeddings that condition generation)

In your [vision_sft_esd_lora_nano.toml](file:///scratch/kcwp264/ESD-WORLD/configs/vision_sft_esd_lora_nano.toml:0:0-0:0), `keys_to_select = ["lora_"]` means **only LoRA adapter weights are updated**. The Qwen3-VL text encoder is completely frozen — it's not in `keys_to_select`, and `keys_to_skip_loading = ["net_ema.", "lora_"]` means base weights are loaded as-is.

In the official full SFT recipe (`vision_sft_nano.toml`), `keys_to_select = ["moe_gen", "time_embedder", "vae2llm", "llm2vae"]` — still **does not include the VLM**. The text encoder is frozen even in full SFT.

**The problem**: The SFT dataloader (`sft_dataset.py:66-95`) requires a caption for every `t2w_window`. If [_select_caption()](file:///scratch/kcwp264/cosmos-framework/cosmos_framework/data/generator/local_datasets/sft_dataset.py:65:0-94:60) returns `None` (no caption key found), the sample is skipped. You can't just omit captions — the model needs text embeddings to condition generation, even if the text encoder itself isn't trained.

## Three viable approaches

### Approach A: Use a generic placeholder caption for all videos

Set every `t2w_window`'s caption to a fixed string like `"Endoscopic submucosal dissection procedure."` The frozen text encoder produces a constant embedding. The generation pathway learns to generate video conditioned on this fixed text + the visual tokens from input frames.

- **Pros**: Simplest fix, no annotation pipeline needed, effectively becomes a video-prediction model
- **Cons**: Model can't do text-conditioned generation at inference (no prompt control); all videos get the same text conditioning, limiting diversity
- **Best for**: Pure Video2World (predict future frames from past frames) — which is likely what you want for an ESD world model

### Approach B: Switch to Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B + Cosmos-H-Surgical

This is a **different, lighter architecture** that's much better suited to your 3× L40S setup:

| | Cosmos3-Nano (current) | Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B |
|---|---|---|
| **Params** | 16B | 2B |
| **Text encoder** | Qwen3-VL-8B (frozen) | Cosmos-Reason1 (frozen) |
| **Architecture** | MoT DiT | Flow-matching DiT |
| **VRAM (LoRA, 3× L40S)** | ~25-30GB/GPU (tight) | ~12-15GB/GPU (comfortable) |
| **Surgical fine-tune exists?** | No | **Yes — Cosmos-H-Surgical** |
| **LoRA rank used in surgical** | N/A | 32 (broader targets) |

**Cosmos-H-Surgical** is already fine-tuned on surgical video (SATA dataset: 2,447 clips, 300k frames across 8 procedures). You could:

1. **Use it as-is** for ESD video generation (it has surgical domain knowledge)
2. **Further fine-tune it** on your ESD videos (transfer from general surgical → ESD-specific)
3. The Cosmos-Predict2.5 LoRA config supports `conditional_frames_probs={2: 1.0}` — pure Video2World mode where text is secondary

The LoRA post-training for Cosmos-Predict2.5 uses:
```python
lora_rank=32,
lora_alpha=32,
lora_target_modules="q_proj,k_proj,v_proj,output_proj,mlp.layer1,mlp.layer2",
conditional_frames_probs={0: 0.333, 1: 0.333, 2: 0.334},  # Train all 3 modes
```

You could set `{2: 1.0}` to train pure Video2World (past frames → future frames), minimizing the need for good captions.

### Approach C: Use a VLM to auto-annotate your ESD videos

This is your second idea — and it's exactly the right instinct. The SATA dataset used **expert human annotations**, but you can automate this with a VLM:

1. **Use Qwen3-VL-8B** (the Reasoner in Cosmos3-Nano, already on your system) to watch each ESD video clip and generate structured JSON captions
2. The `structured_caption.py` module in cosmos-framework defines the exact schema: `subjects`, `background_setting`, `lighting`, `cinematography`, `actions`, `segments`, `temporal_caption`, etc.
3. The prompt upsampling pipeline (`prompts.py`) shows the detailed prompt template for generating these structured captions

This gives you the **best of both worlds**: real text conditioning for the model AND no manual annotation effort.

---

# Can You Use Cosmos-H-Surgical's Reasoner to Annotate?

**Cosmos-H-Surgical is a video generator, not a captioner.** It generates surgical video — it doesn't describe existing video.

But you have two better options for auto-annotation:

1. **Qwen3-VL-8B** (already available as the Cosmos3-Nano Reasoner) — feed it frames from each ESD clip and ask it to generate a structured JSON caption following the `StructuredCaption` schema. This is a standard VLM captioning task.

2. **Cosmos-Reason1** (the text encoder in Cosmos-Predict2.5) — also a VLM that can caption video.

The workflow would be:
```
For each ESD video clip:
  1. Sample ~8 keyframes
  2. Feed to Qwen3-VL with the structured caption prompt template
  3. Get back JSON with subjects, lighting, actions, segments, etc.
  4. Add as `caption_json` to the t2w_window in your JSONL
```

This is exactly how NVIDIA generates the `caption_json` field in the BridgeData2 dataset that the official recipes train on.

Here's the key insight from the official docs:

# Does Cosmos Need Text Captions to Train?

## Yes, the dataloader requires captions — but they can be trivially simple

The critical finding from NVIDIA's own post-training docs:

```bash
# NVIDIA's official example: ONE generic caption for ALL videos
python -m scripts.create_prompts_for_nemo_assets \
    --dataset_path datasets/cosmos_nemo_assets \
    --prompt "A video of sks teal robot."
```

**Cosmos-NeMo-Assets has 4 long videos, all sharing a single caption: `"A video of sks teal robot."`** — and this is an official NVIDIA post-training example. The model trains successfully with this.

## How it works architecturally

```
Training sample:
  text prompt (frozen text encoder) → text embeddings (constant if same caption)
  input frames (1-2 frames)         → visual conditioning
  target frames (future frames)     → ground truth for flow-matching loss

Loss = MSE(predicted_velocity, ground_truth_velocity)
       conditioned on (text_embeddings + input_frame_latents)
```

- **Text encoder**: Frozen (Qwen3-VL-8B for Cosmos3-Nano, Cosmos-Reason1 for Predict2.5). Never trained.
- **Text embeddings**: If you use the same caption for all videos, this becomes a **constant vector** — essentially a bias term. The model ignores it and learns from the visual frames instead.
- **Real conditioning**: For Video2World (V2V), the model gets 2+ input frames and predicts the next frames. The visual conditioning is what matters — text is secondary.

## In the Cosmos3-Nano SFT pipeline specifically

The dataloader (`sft_dataset.py:66-95`) checks for caption keys in this priority:
1. `caption_json` (structured JSON) → serialized to JSON string
2. `qwen3_32b_rewrite-dense` → dense prose
3. `caption` → dense prose (what your JSONL has)
4. Other caption types → weighted random

If **none** found → returns `None` → sample is **skipped**. So you must have *some* caption, but it doesn't need to be good.

## What this means for your ESD training

You have **three options**, from simplest to best:

### Option 1: Generic placeholder (fastest, proven by NVIDIA)
Set every `t2w_window.caption` to `"Endoscopic submucosal dissection procedure."` — a single constant caption for all 59 clips. This is exactly what NVIDIA does with Cosmos-NeMo-Assets. The text encoder produces a constant embedding. The model learns pure Video2World: past frames → future frames.

### Option 2: Light per-procedure captions (minimal effort)
Use 3-4 different captions based on ESD phase:
- `"ESD procedure, marking phase with electrocautery."`
- `"ESD procedure, circumferential incision with electrosurgical knife."`
- `"ESD procedure, submucosal dissection with IT knife."`
- `"ESD procedure, hemostasis with coagulation forceps."`

This gives the model minimal text variation without needing detailed annotations.

### Option 3: VLM auto-annotation (best quality)
Use Qwen3-VL-8B (already on your system) to generate structured `caption_json` for each clip. This gives real text conditioning — you could ask the model at inference "show me an ESD dissection with bleeding control" and get relevant generation.

## The bottom line

**The text encoder is already frozen.** You're never training it. The caption is just an input to a frozen encoder that produces embeddings. Whether those embeddings come from a brilliant structured JSON or a generic string barely matters for Video2World — the model is primarily learning to predict future frames from past frames.

NVIDIA's own recipe proves this: they train on 4 videos with a single 7-word caption. Your 59 clips with a generic ESD caption will work fine.

# Check field completeness across all 140

required_fields = ['subjects', 'background_setting', 'lighting', 'aesthetics', 'cinematography',

print(f'Hallucinated subjects: {sum(1 for i in issues if \"Hallucinated\" in i)}')

print()
print(f'=== Unique subject descriptions ({len(all_subjects)}) ===')
for s in sorted(all_subjects):
    print(f'  - {s}')

**Domain accuracy**: The VLM correctly distinguishes ESD phases (marking → injection → dissection) with phase-appropriate subjects, lighting descriptions ("central hot spot with dim peripheral illumination"), and camera motion ("slow forward movement" for dissection vs "static" for marking).

**Media metadata is correct**: Multiple resolutions (720x576, 1920x1080, 1280x720, 512x512) and FPS values (25, 30, 50, 60) properly extracted per-video — the "210 resolution issues" were just my check assuming all were 720x576, but the variety is correct.

**Phase distribution**: dissection (80), marking (40), injection (20) — matches the ESD procedure workflow.

### Planner Response

Here's the key finding:

## NVIDIA's Official Pipeline vs What We Did

### What NVIDIA does (official pipeline)

1. **VLM watches the actual video** — `cosmos_framework.scripts.caption_from_video` sends video files to a vLLM server via `file://` paths. The VLM (Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct-FP8 or Cosmos-Reason1-7B) **sees the video frames** and generates captions from scratch.

2. **Two-phase process** using the official `video_captioner.txt` prompt:
   - **Phase 1**: VLM watches video → produces structured JSON scene analysis (`<scene_draft>`)
   - **Phase 2**: VLM rewrites into dense narrative (`<final_prompt>`)
   - Both outputs saved: `caption.json` (structured) + `caption.txt` (dense)

3. **Cosmos Curator pipeline** (for raw video → training dataset):
   - TransNetV2 splits videos into semantic clips
   - Cosmos-Reason1-7B or Qwen3-VL captions each clip
   - T5-XXL embeddings pre-computed
   - Sharded into webdataset format

4. **Recommended VLM**: `Qwen/Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct-FP8` — same model family we used, just FP8 quantized.

### What we did

- Took **existing dense text captions** (already in the JSONL) and asked Qwen3-VL-8B to **restructure them** into `caption_json` — **without the VLM seeing the video**.
- Used our own prompt template instead of the official `video_captioner.txt`.
- One-phase: text → structured JSON (no video watching, no two-phase process).

### Key difference

| | Our approach | NVIDIA's approach |
|---|---|---|
| **Input** | Dense text caption | Actual video frames |
| **VLM sees video?** | No | Yes |
| **Schema** | Close match (our prompt was based on [structured_caption.py](file:///scratch/kcwp264/cosmos-framework/cosmos_framework/inference/structured_caption.py:0:0-0:0)) | Official `video_captioner.txt` with richer fields (clothing, gender, age, pose, orientation, relative_size, etc.) |
| **Output** | `caption_json` only | Both `caption.json` + `caption.txt` |
| **Model** | Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct (bf16) | Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct-FP8 (or Cosmos-Reason1-7B) |

### Would using the official pipeline be better?

**Yes, potentially** — because the VLM would see the actual surgical video and could extract visual details (instrument types, tissue appearance, bleeding, exact camera motion) that might be missing or imprecise in the dense text captions. The official prompt also has richer fields (orientation, pose, relative_size, lens_focal_length, artistic_style) that our simplified schema omitted.

**However**, for our case the dense captions were already quite detailed (they describe ESD phase, instruments, lighting, camera motion), and our VLM correctly extracted surgical domain terminology with no hallucinations. The structured captions are valid and will work for training.

**If you want to upgrade**: we could run the official `caption_from_video.py` script on the 59 ESD videos using the L40S you have. This would give video-grounded captions from the official prompt template. Want me to set that up?
